Steady bubbles and drops in inviscid fluids
Authors: David Meyer, Lukas Niebel, Christian Seis
Published: 2025

ABSTRACT
We construct steady non-spherical bubbles and drops, which are traveling wave solutions to the axisymmetric two-phase Euler equations with surface tension, whose inner phase is a bounded connected domain. The solutions have a uniform vorticity distribution in this inner phase and they have a vortex sheet on its surface. Our construction relies on a perturbative approach around an explicit spherical solution, given by Hill’s vortex enclosed by a spherical vortex sheet. The construction is sensitive to the Weber numbers describing the flow. At critical Weber numbers, we perform a bifurcation analysis utilizing the Crandall–Rabinowitz theorem in Sobolev spaces on the 2-sphere. Away from these critical numbers, our construction relies on the implicit function theorem. Our results imply that the model containing surface tension is richer than the ordinary one-phase Euler equations, in the sense that for the latter, Hill’s spherical vortex is unique (modulo translations) among all axisymmetric simply connected uniform vortices of a given circulation.

Introduction

Motivation

Studying the motion and shapes of bubbles and drops in liquids is a fundamental problem in fluid dynamics. It has received considerable attention over more than half a century both by theoretical physicists and experimentalists. The goal of the present work is to lay some of their studies on sound mathematical ground by rigorously constructing equilibrium bubble and drop solutions to the underlying mathematical model equations.

Asymptotic analyses of nearly spherical bubbles and drops in the physics literature have proceeded largely in parallel. By a bubble, we mean a nearly spherical volume of gas immersed in a liquid. By a drop in a liquid, we refer to a nearly spherical volume of one liquid inside another immiscible liquid of comparable density (and viscosity). We will focus on high Reynolds number flows, which will allow us to consider the two-phase Euler equations with surface tension as the underlying model equations.

When a drop or a bubble starts moving by the influence of gravity, the resting ambient liquid induces vorticity on its boundary. The vorticity is subsequently diffused both inwards and outwards of the drop or bubble, and it homogenizes at high Reynolds numbers [11, 51]: In the interior, it generates an inner circulation of uniform vorticity distribution, which is negligible in the case of a bubble, while in an infinite ambient fluid, the flow becomes potential. Thin viscous boundary layers that typically emerge along the surfaces [30, 46] formally disappear in the high-Reynolds number limit. Instead, a vortex sheet forms due to a discontinuity in the tangential velocity components, with its motion driven entirely by circulation rather than gravitational effects. We analyze both scenarios where the circulation inside the vortex body is aligned with and opposite to the circulation on the vortex sheet at the boundary.

By choosing an inviscid model, we also discard any viscous wakes inside and past the moving objects. Because we gain time reversibility, we may even assume symmetry with respect to the co-moving reference plane. Considering inviscid models for moving bubbles and drops in liquids at high Reynolds numbers was justified for instance in [30, 46, 50]. The behavior of bubbles and drops in liquids of intermediate or small Reynolds numbers can be significantly different from that in high Reynolds number flows, see for instance [40].

The two-phase Euler equations describing the translating motion of a bubble or a drop allow for exactly two dimensionless parameters. These are the commonly used Weber number \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {We}$$\end{document}We that measures the inertial forces relative to surface tension forces, and a second quantity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma $$\end{document}γ measuring the vortex strength relative to surface tension forces, see (1.20) below. The latter is sometimes referred to as the vortex Weber number, see, e.g., [33].

In the situation in which the two dimensionless parameters agree, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {We}=\gamma $$\end{document}We=γ, the two-phase Euler equations allow for a spherical traveling wave solution: As the vorticity is uniform, the interior flow inside a sphere must be identical to that inside Hill’s spherical vortex [34]. The exterior flow is potential, and there will be a jump discontinuity of the tangential velocity across the surface if the mass densities of the inner and outer phases are different. This traveling wave solution is thus a spherical vortex sheet enclosing Hill’s vortex. It will be a central object in our analysis. In order to simplify our language use, we will refer to this object as the spherical vortex.

In the present paper, we rigorously construct slightly non-spherical bubbles and drops in situations when the two Weber numbers are close to each other, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {We}\approx \gamma $$\end{document}We≈γ. On the one hand, we find critical values \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\gamma _k)_{k\in \mathbb {N}}$$\end{document}(γk)k∈N at which such objects with identical parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {We}=\gamma $$\end{document}We=γ bifurcate along a curve from the spherical vortex at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _k$$\end{document}γk. On the other hand, away from these critical Weber numbers, we find for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma $$\end{document}γ a small open neighborhood of steady non-spherical solutions with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {We}\not =\gamma $$\end{document}We≠γ. Moreover, we will establish that our non-spherical vortex configurations converge towards a spherical vortex in the limit of vanishing Weber numbers, which is realized in the limit of large surface tensions. The latter applies also to situations, in which either the inner or the outer phase is a vacuum. If the inner phase is a vacuum, our configurations are hollow vortices. Our asymptotic results for small Weber numbers confirm earlier predictions in the physics literature on the spheroidal shapes, for instance, [31, 45, 50], which are based on formal asymptotics.

We let the unit normal vector n point from the inner phase into the outer phase, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nu $$\end{document}ν is then the velocity of the surface in the direction of that normal. While the normal velocity component is necessarily continuous across the interface (1.4), its tangential components will, in general, experience jump discontinuities, turning the surface \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}(t)$$\end{document}S(t) into a vortex sheet. Indeed, a short exercise reveals that the distributional vorticity has a singular part given by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\llbracket {n\times U}\rrbracket }$$\end{document}〚n×U〛, concentrated on the surface.

Our goal in this paper is the construction of traveling wave solutions with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}(t)$$\end{document}S(t) close to a steadily translating sphere of some fixed speed \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V \ge 0$$\end{document}V≥0 and with vorticity distributed both in the interior \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {D}^{\text {in}}(t)$$\end{document}Din(t) and on its boundary \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}(t)$$\end{document}S(t). Before specifying our mathematical setting, we provide a short overview of the related mathematical literature.

We are concerned with a free boundary problem for the Euler equations, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}(t)$$\end{document}S(t) is the free boundary. In the absence of surface tension, it is well known that the (non-steady) interface problem between two inviscid and incompressible fluids is ill-posed due to the Kelvin–Helmholtz instability [25, 63]; however, steady solutions are known to exist [7, 28, 43]. The two-phase Euler equations with surface tension attracted considerable attention in the past years, primarily in the context of studying water waves. In the irrotational case, that is, if the curl of the velocity vector is zero within the two phases and the vorticity is thus concentrated on the interface, the model can be completely reduced to the interface evolution. Local well-posedness results for the two-phase Euler equations with surface tension were obtained in [3, 4, 17, 19, 35, 53]. In [55] short-time regularity of the velocity and the interface is proven. Singularity formation in finite time is shown e.g. in [16, 20].

Regarding the mathematically rigorous phenomenological study of three-dimensional vortex structures, there is a considerable number of results in the one-fluid setting. An important example is Hill’s explicit solution for a spherical vortex [34] from 1894, which we recall in detail and extend to the two-fluid setting below. Given a fixed radius and circulation, it was proven in [6] to be unique modulo translations. We refer to [18], where stability with respect to axisymmetric perturbations is derived. The introduction of that paper also gives an extensive overview of the literature on Hill’s spherical vortex. Vortex rings, which are toroidal vortex configurations that are also observed to emerge out of rising air bubbles as a result of gravity [13, 41], were mathematically constructed in [5, 49], and the leapfrogging interaction or vortex rings was recently investigated in [15, 23]. More general vortex filaments are studied in [36]. Traveling ring-shaped vortex sheets in the one-fluid setting and toroidal vortex bubbles in the two-phase setting were recently constructed in [43]. In the low Reynolds number setting, equilibrium configurations of falling drops were constructed in [12, 26, 57]. Travelling wave solutions were also constructed in the context of water waves with surface tensions [22, 24, 37, 44, 54, 58–60]. The phenomenon in which steady fluid equilibria bifurcate as surface tension varies occurs in many other contexts; see, for instance, [10, 22, 44, 48, 59, 62].

Traveling wave solutions

In order to provide a first formulation of our results, we introduce our notion of traveling wave solutions. Upon a rotation of the coordinates system, we may assume that our bubbles or drops move at speed \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V{\ge 0}$$\end{document}V≥0 in the direction of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_3$$\end{document}x3, assuming that the speed is non-negative is non-restrictive because of the time-reversibility of the problem. We aim to construct traveling wave solutions in the class of axisymmetric and swirl-free velocity fields. As can be easily checked, the curl of such vector fields points into the azimuthal direction. We restrict our attention to configurations that feature a uniform vorticity distribution in the interior of the drop or bubble, which means that the vorticity vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega _a=\textrm{curl} \,u$$\end{document}ωa=curlu in the interior is given by1.11\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \omega _a = {\frac{15}{2}}a \begin{pmatrix} -x_2 \\ x_1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} \text {in }\mathcal {D}^{\text {in}}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ωa=152a-x2x10inDin,for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a \in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}a∈R. The prefactor 15/2 is introduced here to have a simpler notation later on. We remark that (1.11) states that the azimuthal potential vorticity is constant, which is a reasonable assumption, because its value is an integral of the Euler equations, see [42, Section 2.3.3]. In the ambient fluid, on the other hand, we assume that the flow is irrotational, that is1.12\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textrm{curl} \,u=0\quad \text { in }\mathcal {D}^{\text {out}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}curlu=0inDout.It will be convenient to introduce the vector stream function rather than the axisymmetric scalar stream function to represent the velocity field, see, for instance, [42, Section 2.4]. We write1.13\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} u = \textrm{curl} \,\psi -Ve_3, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}u=curlψ-Ve3,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi :\mathbb {R}^3 \rightarrow \mathbb {R}^3$$\end{document}ψ:R3→R3 is a divergence-free vector field. Because u is axisymmetric without swirl, the identity simplifies to\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ u = - \partial _z \psi _\varphi e_r + \left( \frac{1}{r}\psi _{\varphi } +\partial _r\psi _{\varphi }-V\right) e_z,\quad \text{ and }\quad \partial _z \psi _r = \partial _r\psi _z $$\end{document}u=-∂zψφer+1rψφ+∂rψφ-Vez,and∂zψr=∂rψzwhere \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi = \psi _r e_r+\psi _z e_z + \psi _\varphi e_\varphi $$\end{document}ψ=ψrer+ψzez+ψφeφ in cylindrical coordinates \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(r,z,\varphi ) \in [0,\infty ) \times \mathbb {R}\times [0,2\pi )$$\end{document}(r,z,φ)∈[0,∞)×R×[0,2π). Using the divergence-free condition for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi $$\end{document}ψ, which reads \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _r \psi _r + r^{-1}\psi _r +\partial _z \psi _z=0$$\end{document}∂rψr+r-1ψr+∂zψz=0 in cylindrical coordinates, implies that we can choose the vector stream function purely azimuthal, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi _r=\psi _z=0$$\end{document}ψr=ψz=0. The azimuthal function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r\psi _{\varphi }$$\end{document}rψφ coincides thus with the axisymmetric stream function.

The tangential flow condition (1.9) on the velocity finally yields that the interface \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}$$\end{document}S is a stream surface, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r\psi _{\varphi }- r^2 V/2 = \textrm{const}$$\end{document}rψφ-r2V/2=const. There is thus no loss of generality to suppose that1.14\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \psi = \frac{V}{2}re_{\varphi }\quad \text{ on } \mathcal {S}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ψ=V2reφonS.Using the identity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{curl} \,\textrm{curl} \,= \nabla \nabla \cdot -\Delta $$\end{document}curlcurl=∇∇·-Δ, we eventually notice that the vector stream function solves the elliptic problem1.15\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} -\Delta \psi = \omega _a \mathbbm {1}_{\mathcal {D}^{\text {in}}}\quad \text{ in } \mathbb {R}^3 \setminus \mathcal {S}\text{. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}-Δψ=ωa1DininR3\S.Moreover, invoking the representation (1.13), we further rewrite the jump condition (1.10) as1.16\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{1}{2}{\llbracket {\rho |\textrm{curl} \,\psi -Ve_3|^2}\rrbracket }+{\sigma } H=\textrm{const}\quad \text{ on } \mathcal {S}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}12〚ρ|curlψ-Ve3|2〛+σH=constonS.We remark that (1.14), (1.15), and (1.16) constitute an overdetermined boundary problem. Indeed, the elliptic problem (1.15) is posed on the interior and exterior domain equipped with both Dirichlet boundary conditions (1.14) and the jump condition (1.16) for the curl of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi $$\end{document}ψ. For arbitrary domains \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {D}^{\text {in}}$$\end{document}Din, both conditions might not be simultaneously verified. Requiring both conditions simultaneously thus imposes a restriction on the shape of the domain. Our goal in the present paper is to find a surface \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}$$\end{document}S, so that the combined problem (1.14), (1.15), and (1.16) admits a solution. Once this solution is found, the velocity field u defined in (1.13) is a solution to the Euler system (1.6), (1.7), (1.8), and (1.9).

As announced in the first subsection, one explicit solution can be found for spherical surfaces \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}=\mathbb {S}^2_R$$\end{document}S=SR2, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {S}^2_R$$\end{document}SR2 denotes the 2-sphere of radius R. Here, the interior solution describes a spherical vortex derived already by Hill in 1894 [34], and the outer flow is potential. The spherical solution is given by1.17\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \psi _{\textrm{S}}(x) = \begin{pmatrix} -x_2 \\ x_1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} \cdot {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} {\frac{3a}{4}}\left( R^2-\left|x\right|^2\right) +\frac{V_{\textrm{S}}}{2}& \text{ for } \left|x\right|\le R \\ \frac{V_{\textrm{S}}}{2} \frac{R^3}{\left|x\right|^{3}}& \text{ for } \left|x\right|> R, \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ψS(x)=-x2x10·3a4R2-x2+VS2forx≤RVS2R3x3forx>R,where, motivated by (1.14), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_{\textrm{S}}$$\end{document}VS denotes the speed of the vortex configuration. It can be easily checked that this function solves the Dirichlet problem (1.14), (1.15) for any choice of velocity. The precise value of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_{\textrm{S}}$$\end{document}VS is determined by the jump condition (1.16), which in spherical coordinates reads1.18\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{1}{2}{\llbracket {\rho |\textrm{curl} \,\psi _{\textrm{S}}-V_{\textrm{S}}e_3|^2}\rrbracket } =\frac{9}{8}\left( a^2 R^4 \rho ^{\text {in}}- \rho ^{\text {out}}V_{\textrm{S}}^2\right) \sin ^2 \theta . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}12〚ρ|curlψS-VSe3|2〛=98a2R4ρin-ρoutVS2sin2θ.We explain our notational conventions at the end of this first section, see page 10. The function in (1.17) defines a solution to (1.14), (1.15), and (1.16) precisely if1.19\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} V_{\textrm{S}}= \left|a\right| R^2\sqrt{\frac{\rho ^{\text {in}}}{\rho ^{\text {out}}}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}VS=aR2ρinρout.The occurrence of the modulus in this relation is interesting: It shows that there exist spherical solutions traveling in the direction of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_3$$\end{document}x3 (so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_{\textrm{S}}>0$$\end{document}VS>0) both with positive and negative inner circulations a. A negative inner circulation has then to be compensated by a counter-rotating outside flow, so that the overall circulation is again positive. In fact, a short computation of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U = \textrm{curl} \,\psi _{\textrm{S}}$$\end{document}U=curlψS shows that the function in (1.17) generates a vortex sheet on the surface if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_{\textrm{S}}\not =a R^2$$\end{document}VS≠aR2. In particular, even in the one-fluid setting, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho ^{\text {in}}=\rho ^{\text {out}}$$\end{document}ρin=ρout, a spherical vortex sheet solution exists if (1.19) is satisfied for a negative a. For a positive and equal densities, the velocity field is continuous and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi _{\textrm{S}}$$\end{document}ψS is precisely the vector stream function that corresponds to Hill’s spherical vortex in the one-fluid setting.

In Section 2, we will non-dimensionalize the problem (1.14), (1.15), and (1.16). As mentioned in the introduction, we will see that all system constants can be reduced to two dimensionless control parameters: the Weber number \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {We}$$\end{document}We and the vortex Weber number \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma $$\end{document}γ, given by1.20\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \operatorname {We}=\frac{\rho ^{\text {out}}V^2R }{\sigma } , \quad \gamma = \frac{\rho ^{\text {in}}a^2R^5 }{\sigma } . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}We=ρoutV2Rσ,γ=ρina2R5σ.For near-spherical vortices, R denotes their average radius. The first quantity measures the inertial forces relative to the surface tension forces, while the second one measures the vortex intensity relative to the surface tension forces. It is readily checked that condition (1.19) under which the spherical function (1.17) solves the jump condition (1.16) can now be equivalently stated as1.21\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \operatorname {We}= \gamma . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}We=γ.In this paper, we will look for solutions to the overdetermined problem (1.14), (1.15), and (1.16) which are close to the spherical one in (1.17), (1.19). More precisely, we will consider surfaces that can be written as a graph over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {S}^2_R$$\end{document}SR2, i.e., for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta \in \operatorname {C}^0(\mathbb {S}_R^2)$$\end{document}η∈C0(SR2) we set1.22\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {S}=\mathcal {S}_\eta = \left\{ (1+\eta (x))x : x \in \mathbb {S}^2_R \right\} . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}S=Sη=(1+η(x))x:x∈SR2.This is the boundary of a simply connected bounded set if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta $$\end{document}η is continuous and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta >-1$$\end{document}η>-1 (which is always going to be the case below). Any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}_\eta $$\end{document}Sη for which (1.14), (1.15), and (1.16) are simultaneously solvable yields a traveling wave solution to the two-phase Euler equations.

We focus on axisymmetric functions that experience a reflection symmetry with respect to the reference plane. For simplicity, we call such functions simply symmetric. Then f is symmetric precisely if1.23\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} f = f(\theta )\quad \text{ and }\quad f\left( \frac{\pi }{2}-\theta \right) =f\left( \frac{\pi }{2}+\theta \right) . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}f=f(θ)andfπ2-θ=fπ2+θ.In this notation, symmetric functions are \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi $$\end{document}π-periodic functions of the polar angle \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta $$\end{document}θ.

We give now the first version of our main result.

Whether the constructed objects are bubbles or drops depends on the particular choices of the densities \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho ^{\text {in}}$$\end{document}ρin and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho ^{\text {out}}$$\end{document}ρout.

Uniqueness even holds true in a large class of Sobolev functions, which we will explain in a more precise version of Theorem 1.1, namely Theorem 2.1 in Section 2. Corollary 1.2

There exist values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma $$\end{document}γ close to the bifurcation set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma $$\end{document}Γ for which non-spherical steady vortex solutions with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {We}=\gamma $$\end{document}We=γ exist. In particular, for these values, the spherical vortex is non-unique.

This result is in stark contrast with what is known in the one-fluid setting, where no surface tension is present. There, Hill’s spherical vortex solution is unique in the sense that for any given speed V and vorticity a, the only solution to the corresponding elliptic problem, namely (1.14), (1.15), and (1.16) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho ^{\text {in}}=\rho ^{\text {out}}$$\end{document}ρin=ρout and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma =0$$\end{document}σ=0, is a translation of Hill’s vortex, see [6, Theorem 1.1].

Remark 1.3

Some remarks on Theorem 1.1 are in order. For infinite surface tension, we deduce from the jump condition (1.16) that the only admissible solution is a surface of constant mean curvature, which in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^3$$\end{document}R3 is a sphere. Our asymptotics (1.24) moreover indicate that any non-spherical steady solution near the sphere approaches the spherical shape in the limit \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma \rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}σ→∞.The asymptotics that we find for the shape function in the limit of small Weber numbers (1.24) agrees with the formal predictions made by Pozrikidis [50] in the case of negligible inner circulation or density, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma =0$$\end{document}γ=0. In this situation, we are concerned with a hollow vortex.To classify the shape of our spheroidal objects, we compute the ratio of the cross-stream axis extension to the parallel axis extension, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \chi = \frac{R+d_{\varepsilon }(\pi /2)}{R+d_\varepsilon (0)} = 1 + \frac{9}{32}\varepsilon (\delta ^{\text {out}}-\delta ^{\text {in}}) +o(\varepsilon ), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}χ=R+dε(π/2)R+dε(0)=1+932ε(δout-δin)+o(ε), as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon \ll 1$$\end{document}ε≪1. Hence, the spheroid is oblate if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta ^{\text {out}}>\delta ^{\text {in}}$$\end{document}δout>δin and prolate if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta ^{\text {out}}<\delta ^{\text {in}}$$\end{document}δout<δin. This observation is consistent with the physics literature on bubbles and drops [31]. The resulting shapes are visualized in Figure 1. In particular, if the inner circulation or density is negligible as for bubbles, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma =\delta ^{\text {in}}=0$$\end{document}γ=δin=0, and the Weber number is small \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {We}=\varepsilon \delta ^{\text {out}}\ll 1$$\end{document}We=εδout≪1, the surface \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}$$\end{document}S is always oblate, and we have to leading order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \chi = 1+ \frac{9}{32} \operatorname {We}+ \mathcal {O}(\operatorname {We}^2), $$\end{document}χ=1+932We+O(We2), as predicted, for instance, in [31, 45] for high Reynolds number flows. Actually, as pointed out by Harper [31, p. 77] in spite of the limitation of this statement to small Weber numbers, “oblate spheroids are found to be a fair approximation to the true shapes of bubbles for quite large values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {We}$$\end{document}We.”Explicitly calculating the critical Weber numbers \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\gamma _k)_{k \in \mathbb {N}}$$\end{document}(γk)k∈N in Theorem 1.1 seems to be a hard problem. It is related to the spectrum of an infinite Jacobi matrix. Here, an analogy to calculating the spectrum of a discrete Schrödinger operator can be drawn, which is only known in very special cases. Numerically, we obtain the first values as listed in Table 1, which are plotted in Figure 3. The first two critical Weber numbers match the prediction in [50], where the Weber number is defined with a factor of 2 difference from ours. The rigorous lower bound on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _1$$\end{document}γ1 in Theorem 1.1 is not optimal.As we cannot explicitly calculate the values \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\gamma _k)_{k \in \mathbb {N}}$$\end{document}(γk)k∈N nor the corresponding solutions of the linear problem we cannot obtain the asymptotic of the bifurcation curve constructed in Theorem 1.1 explicitly. We refer to Remark 4.2 for details. Relying on numerical approximations, we are able to provide the schematics in Figure 2 for the first two bifurcations.It is an interesting open question whether, outside of the perturbative setting, traveling wave solutions exist. We believe, however, that such solutions would be unstable and thus not physically relevant. For instance, in the case of very large air bubbles (corresponding to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {We}\gg 1$$\end{document}We≫1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma =0$$\end{document}γ=0), experiments show that the fluid flow becomes unsteady and turbulent, see for instance [61].A natural question concerns the global continuation of the solution branches constructed here. If the local curve given by the implicit function theorem terminates, one expects that some geometric or analytic quantity must blow up (e.g., the curvature of the interface, or the velocity at the boundary). Likewise, if a branch obtained via the Crandall–Rabinowitz theorem cannot be extended, its endpoint should correspond to a singular limiting configuration. In abstract global bifurcation theory, see [32, Theorem 4.5], a branch of solutions can either be continued indefinitely or else must terminate in a singular state. Applying such results in our setting would require verifying delicate Fredholm properties of the linearized operator along the branch, which is a highly nontrivial task. We therefore leave a rigorous global analysis to future work. Moreover, we emphasize that numerically tracking the bifurcation diagram and identifying possible termination scenarios seems to be an interesting open problem.We finally remark that for small \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {We}$$\end{document}We, the main effect in the equations is due to the mean curvature. In principle, one could also prove an analogous version of the theorem for small Weber number and small internal circulation for other interior vorticity distributions (or even other governing equations, as long as the stationary equations for a fixed domain are solvable and behave well under perturbations of the boundary).

For infinite surface tension, we deduce from the jump condition (1.16) that the only admissible solution is a surface of constant mean curvature, which in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^3$$\end{document}R3 is a sphere. Our asymptotics (1.24) moreover indicate that any non-spherical steady solution near the sphere approaches the spherical shape in the limit \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma \rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}σ→∞.

For infinite surface tension, we deduce from the jump condition (1.16) that the only admissible solution is a surface of constant mean curvature, which in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^3$$\end{document}R3 is a sphere. Our asymptotics (1.24) moreover indicate that any non-spherical steady solution near the sphere approaches the spherical shape in the limit \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma \rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}σ→∞.

The asymptotics that we find for the shape function in the limit of small Weber numbers (1.24) agrees with the formal predictions made by Pozrikidis [50] in the case of negligible inner circulation or density, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma =0$$\end{document}γ=0. In this situation, we are concerned with a hollow vortex.

The asymptotics that we find for the shape function in the limit of small Weber numbers (1.24) agrees with the formal predictions made by Pozrikidis [50] in the case of negligible inner circulation or density, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma =0$$\end{document}γ=0. In this situation, we are concerned with a hollow vortex.

To classify the shape of our spheroidal objects, we compute the ratio of the cross-stream axis extension to the parallel axis extension, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \chi = \frac{R+d_{\varepsilon }(\pi /2)}{R+d_\varepsilon (0)} = 1 + \frac{9}{32}\varepsilon (\delta ^{\text {out}}-\delta ^{\text {in}}) +o(\varepsilon ), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}χ=R+dε(π/2)R+dε(0)=1+932ε(δout-δin)+o(ε), as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon \ll 1$$\end{document}ε≪1. Hence, the spheroid is oblate if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta ^{\text {out}}>\delta ^{\text {in}}$$\end{document}δout>δin and prolate if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta ^{\text {out}}<\delta ^{\text {in}}$$\end{document}δout<δin. This observation is consistent with the physics literature on bubbles and drops [31]. The resulting shapes are visualized in Figure 1. In particular, if the inner circulation or density is negligible as for bubbles, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma =\delta ^{\text {in}}=0$$\end{document}γ=δin=0, and the Weber number is small \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {We}=\varepsilon \delta ^{\text {out}}\ll 1$$\end{document}We=εδout≪1, the surface \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}$$\end{document}S is always oblate, and we have to leading order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \chi = 1+ \frac{9}{32} \operatorname {We}+ \mathcal {O}(\operatorname {We}^2), $$\end{document}χ=1+932We+O(We2), as predicted, for instance, in [31, 45] for high Reynolds number flows. Actually, as pointed out by Harper [31, p. 77] in spite of the limitation of this statement to small Weber numbers, “oblate spheroids are found to be a fair approximation to the true shapes of bubbles for quite large values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {We}$$\end{document}We.”

To classify the shape of our spheroidal objects, we compute the ratio of the cross-stream axis extension to the parallel axis extension, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \chi = \frac{R+d_{\varepsilon }(\pi /2)}{R+d_\varepsilon (0)} = 1 + \frac{9}{32}\varepsilon (\delta ^{\text {out}}-\delta ^{\text {in}}) +o(\varepsilon ), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}χ=R+dε(π/2)R+dε(0)=1+932ε(δout-δin)+o(ε), as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon \ll 1$$\end{document}ε≪1. Hence, the spheroid is oblate if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta ^{\text {out}}>\delta ^{\text {in}}$$\end{document}δout>δin and prolate if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta ^{\text {out}}<\delta ^{\text {in}}$$\end{document}δout<δin. This observation is consistent with the physics literature on bubbles and drops [31]. The resulting shapes are visualized in Figure 1. In particular, if the inner circulation or density is negligible as for bubbles, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma =\delta ^{\text {in}}=0$$\end{document}γ=δin=0, and the Weber number is small \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {We}=\varepsilon \delta ^{\text {out}}\ll 1$$\end{document}We=εδout≪1, the surface \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}$$\end{document}S is always oblate, and we have to leading order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \chi = 1+ \frac{9}{32} \operatorname {We}+ \mathcal {O}(\operatorname {We}^2), $$\end{document}χ=1+932We+O(We2), as predicted, for instance, in [31, 45] for high Reynolds number flows. Actually, as pointed out by Harper [31, p. 77] in spite of the limitation of this statement to small Weber numbers, “oblate spheroids are found to be a fair approximation to the true shapes of bubbles for quite large values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {We}$$\end{document}We.”

Explicitly calculating the critical Weber numbers \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\gamma _k)_{k \in \mathbb {N}}$$\end{document}(γk)k∈N in Theorem 1.1 seems to be a hard problem. It is related to the spectrum of an infinite Jacobi matrix. Here, an analogy to calculating the spectrum of a discrete Schrödinger operator can be drawn, which is only known in very special cases. Numerically, we obtain the first values as listed in Table 1, which are plotted in Figure 3. The first two critical Weber numbers match the prediction in [50], where the Weber number is defined with a factor of 2 difference from ours. The rigorous lower bound on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _1$$\end{document}γ1 in Theorem 1.1 is not optimal.

As we cannot explicitly calculate the values \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\gamma _k)_{k \in \mathbb {N}}$$\end{document}(γk)k∈N nor the corresponding solutions of the linear problem we cannot obtain the asymptotic of the bifurcation curve constructed in Theorem 1.1 explicitly. We refer to Remark 4.2 for details. Relying on numerical approximations, we are able to provide the schematics in Figure 2 for the first two bifurcations.

It is an interesting open question whether, outside of the perturbative setting, traveling wave solutions exist. We believe, however, that such solutions would be unstable and thus not physically relevant. For instance, in the case of very large air bubbles (corresponding to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {We}\gg 1$$\end{document}We≫1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma =0$$\end{document}γ=0), experiments show that the fluid flow becomes unsteady and turbulent, see for instance [61].

It is an interesting open question whether, outside of the perturbative setting, traveling wave solutions exist. We believe, however, that such solutions would be unstable and thus not physically relevant. For instance, in the case of very large air bubbles (corresponding to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {We}\gg 1$$\end{document}We≫1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma =0$$\end{document}γ=0), experiments show that the fluid flow becomes unsteady and turbulent, see for instance [61].

A natural question concerns the global continuation of the solution branches constructed here. If the local curve given by the implicit function theorem terminates, one expects that some geometric or analytic quantity must blow up (e.g., the curvature of the interface, or the velocity at the boundary). Likewise, if a branch obtained via the Crandall–Rabinowitz theorem cannot be extended, its endpoint should correspond to a singular limiting configuration. In abstract global bifurcation theory, see [32, Theorem 4.5], a branch of solutions can either be continued indefinitely or else must terminate in a singular state. Applying such results in our setting would require verifying delicate Fredholm properties of the linearized operator along the branch, which is a highly nontrivial task. We therefore leave a rigorous global analysis to future work. Moreover, we emphasize that numerically tracking the bifurcation diagram and identifying possible termination scenarios seems to be an interesting open problem.

If the local curve given by the implicit function theorem terminates, one expects that some geometric or analytic quantity must blow up (e.g., the curvature of the interface, or the velocity at the boundary). Likewise, if a branch obtained via the Crandall–Rabinowitz theorem cannot be extended, its endpoint should correspond to a singular limiting configuration. In abstract global bifurcation theory, see [32, Theorem 4.5], a branch of solutions can either be continued indefinitely or else must terminate in a singular state. Applying such results in our setting would require verifying delicate Fredholm properties of the linearized operator along the branch, which is a highly nontrivial task. We therefore leave a rigorous global analysis to future work. Moreover, we emphasize that numerically tracking the bifurcation diagram and identifying possible termination scenarios seems to be an interesting open problem.

We finally remark that for small \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {We}$$\end{document}We, the main effect in the equations is due to the mean curvature. In principle, one could also prove an analogous version of the theorem for small Weber number and small internal circulation for other interior vorticity distributions (or even other governing equations, as long as the stationary equations for a fixed domain are solvable and behave well under perturbations of the boundary).

We finally remark that for small \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {We}$$\end{document}We, the main effect in the equations is due to the mean curvature. In principle, one could also prove an analogous version of the theorem for small Weber number and small internal circulation for other interior vorticity distributions (or even other governing equations, as long as the stationary equations for a fixed domain are solvable and behave well under perturbations of the boundary).

The perturbative Ansatz

We aim to study perturbations of the spherical solution (1.17), whose surfaces are close to the sphere of radius R. In order to non-dimensionalise the overdetermined problem (1.14), (1.15), (1.16), we start by noticing that the change of variables\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} x=R \hat{x},\quad \psi = R^3\hat{\psi }, \quad V = R^2\hat{V},\quad \sigma = R^5\hat{\sigma }, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}x=Rx^,ψ=R3ψ^,V=R2V^,σ=R5σ^,allows us to restrict our attention to the case of the unit sphere, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R=1$$\end{document}R=1. We may furthermore decompose the vector stream function into its inner and outer contributions: In the interior, we introduce \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi ^{\text {in}}:\mathcal {D}^{\text {in}}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}^3$$\end{document}ψin:Din→R3 satisfying2.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} -\Delta \psi ^{\text {in}}= \frac{15}{2} s \sin \theta \, e_\varphi & \text{ in } \mathcal {D}^{\text {in}},\\ \psi ^{\text {in}}=0& \text{ on } \mathcal {S}, \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}-Δψin=152ssinθeφinDin,ψin=0onS,and in the outer domain, we let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi ^{\text {out}}:\mathcal {D}^{\text {out}}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}^3$$\end{document}ψout:Dout→R3 be the solution of2.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} -\Delta \psi ^{\text {out}}= 0 & \text{ in } \mathcal {D}^{\text {out}},\\ \psi ^{\text {out}}= \frac{1}{2} s \sin \theta \, e_\varphi & \text{ on } \mathcal {S}, \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}-Δψout=0inDout,ψout=12ssinθeφonS,vanishing at infinity. Regarding the well-posedness of the Laplace equation in exterior domains, we refer the reader to [8]. We note that in the present article, we only consider a small perturbation of the exterior unit ball where we obtain well-posedness by means of the Kelvin transform in the proof of Proposition 3.4.

With the above notation, we write\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \psi = \left( a\psi ^{\text {in}}+\frac{V}{2}s \sin \theta \, e_\varphi \right) \mathbbm {1}_{\mathcal {D}^{\text {in}}}+ V\psi ^{\text {out}}\mathbbm {1}_{\mathcal {D}^{\text {out}}}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ψ=aψin+V2ssinθeφ1Din+Vψout1Dout,for any solution to (1.14),(1.15) where we consider \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}$$\end{document}S (and thus \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {D}^{\text {in}}$$\end{document}Din and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {D}^{\text {out}}$$\end{document}Dout) to be given.

In terms of the new inner and outer stream functions, the jump condition (1.16) then reads2.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{\gamma }{2}|\textrm{curl} \,\psi ^{\text {in}}|^2-\frac{\operatorname {We}}{2}|\textrm{curl} \,\psi ^{\text {out}}-e_3|^2+H= \textrm{const}\quad \text{ on } \mathcal {S}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}γ2|curlψin|2-We2|curlψout-e3|2+H=constonS,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma $$\end{document}γ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {We}$$\end{document}We denote the Weber numbers introduced in (1.20). If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}$$\end{document}S is a sphere, then we recall from (1.21) that the spherical function in (1.17) is a solution to this precisely if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma = \operatorname {We}$$\end{document}γ=We.

As described in the introduction, we pursue a perturbative ansatz. We will study this identity in suitable function spaces in which we mod out constants. Then, finding traveling wave solutions will correspond to constructing zeros of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}$$\end{document}F.
