For any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}k∈N, there exists a unique local curve \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s\mapsto \gamma (s)$$\end{document}s↦γ(s) passing through \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _k$$\end{document}γk and there are associated nontrivial shape functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta (s)\in \mathcal {M}_{c_0}^\beta $$\end{document}η(s)∈Mc0β such that the jump equation (2.6) is solved with Weber numbers \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\gamma (s),\gamma (s))$$\end{document}(γ(s),γ(s)). These shape functions are smooth.

For any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}k∈N, there exists a unique local curve \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s\mapsto \gamma (s)$$\end{document}s↦γ(s) passing through \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _k$$\end{document}γk and there are associated nontrivial shape functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta (s)\in \mathcal {M}_{c_0}^\beta $$\end{document}η(s)∈Mc0β such that the jump equation (2.6) is solved with Weber numbers \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\gamma (s),\gamma (s))$$\end{document}(γ(s),γ(s)). These shape functions are smooth.

In view of the perturbative setting presented in the present section, the above theorem readily implies Theorem 1.1. Notice that our rescaling implies that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d_{\varepsilon } =R\eta _{\varepsilon }$$\end{document}dε=Rηε.

Our analysis is structured as follows. We introduce the correct functional analytic setup in Section 3, where we also prove the Fréchet differentiability of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {J}$$\end{document}J and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {C}$$\end{document}C and give formulas for their derivatives. In Section 4 we provide the proof of Theorem 2.1.

Analysis of the functional \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}$$\end{document}F

In this section, we study the regularity properties of the functional \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}$$\end{document}F and derive and analyze its linearization. First, we introduce the underlying function spaces for functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta :\mathbb {S}^2 \rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}η:S2→R and recall some important facts on Sobolev spaces on the sphere.

Sobolev spaces on the sphere.

We denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {L}^2(\mathbb {S}^{2})$$\end{document}L2(S2) the space of square-integrable functions on the sphere equipped with the uniform measure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\, \textrm{d}\sigma (x) = \sin (\theta ) \, \textrm{d}\varphi \, \textrm{d}\theta $$\end{document}dσ(x)=sin(θ)dφdθ (which is the same as the Hausdorff measure on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {S}^2$$\end{document}S2 up to a constant factor) and we write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \cdot , \cdot \rangle $$\end{document}⟨·,·⟩ for the induced \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {L}^2$$\end{document}L2 scalar product.

Our analysis is based on the fact that spherical harmonics \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{ Y_l^m: l\in \mathbb {N}_0, \, -l \le m \le l \} $$\end{document}{Ylm:l∈N0,-l≤m≤l} form an orthonormal eigenbasis of the Laplace–Beltrami operator on the sphere \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta _{\mathbb {S}^2}$$\end{document}ΔS2 with respect to the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {L}^2(\mathbb {S}^2)$$\end{document}L2(S2) scalar product. The corresponding eigenvalues \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-l(l+1)$$\end{document}-l(l+1) have the multiplicity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2l+1$$\end{document}2l+1. We have the expansion3.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} f=\sum _{l=0}^{\infty } \sum _{m=-l}^{l} \langle f, Y_l^m \rangle Y_{l}^m, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}f=∑l=0∞∑m=-ll⟨f,Ylm⟩Ylm,for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f \in \operatorname {L}^2(\mathbb {S}^{2})$$\end{document}f∈L2(S2). We recall that spherical harmonics can be expressed as3.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} Y_l^m(\theta ,\varphi ) = c_{l,m} P_{l}^m(\cos \theta ) e^{i m \varphi }, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Ylm(θ,φ)=cl,mPlm(cosθ)eimφ,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_{l,m}= \sqrt{\frac{(2 l+1)}{4 \pi } \frac{(l-m)!}{(l+m)!}}$$\end{document}cl,m=(2l+1)4π(l-m)!(l+m)! are positive constants and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P_l^m$$\end{document}Plm are the associated Legendre polynomials. We refer the reader e.g. to [47] for background reading.

For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta >0$$\end{document}β>0, we define the Sobolev space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {H}^\beta (\mathbb {S}^{2})$$\end{document}Hβ(S2) as the space of all functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f \in \operatorname {L}^2(\mathbb {S}^{2})$$\end{document}f∈L2(S2) with\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert f\Vert _{\operatorname {H}^\beta (\mathbb {S}^{2})}^2=\sum _{l = 0}^{\infty } \sum _{m=-l}^{l}\left( 1+l\right) ^{2\beta }\left| \langle f, Y_l^m \rangle \right| ^2<\infty . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖f‖Hβ(S2)2=∑l=0∞∑m=-ll1+l2β⟨f,Ylm⟩2<∞.These spaces can be equivalently defined via smooth charts [29], and thus, they arise as the trace spaces of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {H}^{\beta +\frac{1}{2}}(B_1(0))$$\end{document}Hβ+12(B1(0)), cf. (3.8). For integer exponents \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta \in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}β∈N, they coincide with the classical Sobolev spaces defined via differentiation on the manifold.

For notational convenience, we introduce a subspace of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {H}^\beta (\mathbb {S}^2)$$\end{document}Hβ(S2) that reflects the symmetric setting we restrict to in (1.23).

Definition 3.1

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta \ge 0$$\end{document}β≥0. We define\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \operatorname {H}^\beta _{\textrm{sym}}(\mathbb {S}^2):=\left\{ f \in \operatorname {H}^\beta (\mathbb {S}^2) : f = f(\theta ) \text{ with } f\left( \frac{\pi }{2}-\theta \right) = f\left( \frac{\pi }{2}+\theta \right) \right\} , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Hsymβ(S2):=f∈Hβ(S2):f=f(θ)withfπ2-θ=fπ2+θ,the subspace of all axisymmetric functions in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {H}^\beta (\mathbb {S}^2)$$\end{document}Hβ(S2), which are symmetric in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_3$$\end{document}x3.

The following characterization will be beneficial for our analysis.

Lemma 3.2

For all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta \ge 0$$\end{document}β≥0 we have3.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \operatorname {H}^\beta _{\textrm{sym}}(\mathbb {S}^2) := \left\{ f \in \operatorname {H}^\beta (\mathbb {S}^2) : \langle f, Y^m_l \rangle = 0 \text{ if } \text{ l } \text{ is } \text{ odd } \text{ or } m \not = 0 \right\} . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Hsymβ(S2):=f∈Hβ(S2):⟨f,Ylm⟩=0iflisoddorm≠0.

Proof

In view of the expansion of f in spherical harmonics given in (3.1) and the representation formula (3.2), it is clear that f is a function of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta $$\end{document}θ alone if and only if\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \langle f,Y^{m}_l\rangle =0 \quad \text{ for } \text{ all } m\not =0. $$\end{document}⟨f,Ylm⟩=0forallm≠0.It is thus enough to choose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m=0$$\end{document}m=0, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {L}^2_{\textrm{sym}}(\mathbb {S}^2)$$\end{document}Lsym2(S2) is then spanned by the Legendre polynomials \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P^0_l(\cos \theta )$$\end{document}Pl0(cosθ). The symmetry with respect to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi /2$$\end{document}π/2, which makes f an even \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi $$\end{document}π-periodic function, is then equivalent to requiring that all generating Legendre polynomials are even, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P_l^0(\cos \theta ) = P_l^0(-\cos \theta )$$\end{document}Pl0(cosθ)=Pl0(-cosθ). This is the case precisely if l is even, and thus\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \langle f, Y^{0}_l \rangle =0\quad \text{ for } \text{ any } \text{ odd } \text{ l }. $$\end{document}⟨f,Yl0⟩=0foranyoddl.This gives the desired characterization. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

The following property of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {H}^\beta (\mathbb {S}^2)$$\end{document}Hβ(S2) and thus of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {H}^\beta _{\textrm{sym}}(\mathbb {S}^2)$$\end{document}Hsymβ(S2) is crucial.

Lemma 3.3

For any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \beta >0 $$\end{document}β>0 and all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k \in \mathbb {N}_0$$\end{document}k∈N0, we have the embeddings \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {H}^{\beta +k+1}(\mathbb {S}^2) \hookrightarrow \operatorname {C}^k(\mathbb {S}^2)$$\end{document}Hβ+k+1(S2)↪Ck(S2) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \operatorname {H}_{\textrm{sym}}^{\beta +k+1}(\mathbb {S}^2) \hookrightarrow \operatorname {C}^k(\mathbb {S}^2) $$\end{document}Hsymβ+k+1(S2)↪Ck(S2). In particular, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \operatorname {H}_{\textrm{sym}}^{\beta +1}(\mathbb {S}^2)$$\end{document}Hsymβ+1(S2) is a multiplicative algebra and closed under composition with smooth functions.

Proof

Since being an element of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {H}^\beta (\mathbb {S}^2)$$\end{document}Hβ(S2) or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {C}^\beta (\mathbb {S}^2)$$\end{document}Cβ(S2), respectively, is a local property, which can be checked on open sets that are diffeomorphic to an open set of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^2$$\end{document}R2, the embeddings follow directly from the corresponding Sobolev embeddings on open sets in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^2$$\end{document}R2 and the Leibniz rule. Similarly, the fact that the spaces are closed under composition with smooth functions can be checked on open sets, where it is well-known, see e.g. [14]. For the strategy we refer to the proof of [9, Theorem 2.20] for the case of integer \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta \in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}β∈N and [29] for the tools to make it work in the fractional setting. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Differentiability and linearization

In this section, we explain the differentiability of the functional \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}$$\end{document}F and we compute its derivative at the sphere. Our goal is the following proposition.

Proposition 3.4

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha >0$$\end{document}α>0 be given. There exists a constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_0>0$$\end{document}c0>0 such that the functional\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \mathcal {F}: \mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {R}\times \mathcal {M}_{c_0}^{\alpha +2} (0)\rightarrow \operatorname {H}^{\alpha }(\mathbb {S}^2) /_\textrm{const}$$\end{document}F:R×R×Mc0α+2(0)→Hα(S2)/constis continuously Fréchet differentiable. Its derivative at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\gamma ,\gamma ,0)$$\end{document}(γ,γ,0) is given by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \langle \textrm{D}_{\eta }\mathcal {F}(\gamma ,\gamma ,\eta )|_{\eta =0} ,\delta \eta \rangle = \frac{9}{2} \gamma \sin \theta \,{e_{\varphi }\cdot }(2 {{\,\textrm{Id}\,}}-\Lambda )(\sin \theta \, \delta \eta \, {e_{\varphi }})- \left( \Delta _{\mathbb {S}^2}+2{{\,\textrm{Id}\,}}\right) \delta \eta , $$\end{document}⟨DηF(γ,γ,η)|η=0,δη⟩=92γsinθeφ·(2Id-Λ)(sinθδηeφ)-ΔS2+2Idδη,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda $$\end{document}Λ denotes the Dirichlet-to-Neumann operator of the unit ball.

To start with, we first notice that by Lemma 3.3, there exists for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha > 0$$\end{document}α>0 a radius \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta _{\alpha }$$\end{document}δα such that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \Vert \eta \Vert _\infty +\Vert \nabla _{\mathbb {S}^2} \eta \Vert _\infty < \frac{1}{2} $$\end{document}‖η‖∞+‖∇S2η‖∞<12for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta \in B_{\delta _{\alpha }}^{ \operatorname {H}_{\textrm{sym}}^{\alpha +2} (\mathbb {S}^2)} (0) \subset \operatorname {H}_{\textrm{sym}}^{\alpha +2}(\mathbb {S}^2)$$\end{document}η∈BδαHsymα+2(S2)(0)⊂Hsymα+2(S2).

We introduce our solution manifold and its tangent at the origin.

Lemma 3.5

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha >0$$\end{document}α>0 be given and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_0\le \delta _{\alpha }$$\end{document}c0≤δα. Then the Banach manifold\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {M}^{\alpha +2} := \mathcal {M}^{\alpha +2}_{c_0} = \left\{ \eta \in B_{c_0}^{ \operatorname {H}_{\textrm{sym}}^{\alpha +2}(\mathbb {S}^2)} (0): \left|\mathcal {D}^{\text {in}}_\eta \right| = \frac{4}{3} \pi \right\} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Mα+2:=M0α+2=η∈Bc0Hsymα+2(S2)(0):Dηin=43πis smooth and its tangent space at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta = 0$$\end{document}η=0 is given by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} T_0\mathcal {M}^{\alpha +2} = \left\{ \eta \in \operatorname {H}_{\textrm{sym}}^{\alpha +2}(\mathbb {S}^2) : \int _{\mathbb {S}^2} \eta \, \textrm{d}\sigma = 0\right\} . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}T0Mα+2=η∈Hsymα+2(S2):∫S2ηdσ=0.

Proof

The assertion is fairly well-known. See, for instance, Proposition 3.3 in [43] for details. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

A simple calculation in differential geometry, which we omit here, shows that the pull-back of the mean curvature \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_\eta \circ \chi _\eta $$\end{document}Hη∘χη for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta $$\end{document}η suitably smooth can be written as3.4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {C}(\eta )= H_\eta \circ \chi _\eta =\frac{1}{1+\eta } \left( 2\frac{1+\eta }{\sqrt{g_\eta }} - \frac{\Delta _{\mathbb {S}^2} \eta }{\sqrt{g_\eta }} - \nabla _{\mathbb {S}^2} \frac{1}{\sqrt{g_\eta }} \cdot \nabla _{\mathbb {S}^2} \eta \right) , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}C(η)=Hη∘χη=11+η21+ηgη-ΔS2ηgη-∇S21gη·∇S2η,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g_\eta = (1+\eta )^2+ \left|\nabla _{\mathbb {S}^2} \eta \right|^2$$\end{document}gη=(1+η)2+∇S2η2. See, for instance, [52, Section 2.2] or [26, Section 7] (where a different sign convention is employed) for a derivation.

We provide the differentiability of the curvature term.

Lemma 3.6

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha > 0$$\end{document}α>0. The mapping\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {C}:\mathcal {M}^{\alpha +2} \rightarrow \operatorname {H}_{\textrm{sym}}^{\alpha }(\mathbb {S}^2)/_\textrm{const}\end{aligned}$$\end{document}C:Mα+2→Hsymα(S2)/constis continuously Fréchet-differentiable and3.5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left. \textrm{D}_\eta \, \mathcal {C}(\eta ) \right| _{\eta = 0} = - \left( \Delta _{\mathbb {S}^2}+2{{\,\textrm{Id}\,}}\right) :T_0\mathcal {M}^{\alpha +2} \rightarrow \operatorname {H}_{\textrm{sym}}^{\alpha }(\mathbb {S}^2)/_\textrm{const}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}DηC(η)η=0=-ΔS2+2Id:T0Mα+2→Hsymα(S2)/const.

Proof

It is elementary to check that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_\eta \circ \chi _\eta $$\end{document}Hη∘χη inherits the symmetry properties (1.23) from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta $$\end{document}η. As \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha > 0$$\end{document}α>0, an application of Lemma 3.3 yields that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {H}_{\textrm{sym}}^{\alpha +2}(\mathbb {S}^2)$$\end{document}Hsymα+2(S2) is an algebra embedding into \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \operatorname {C}^1(\mathbb {S}^2)$$\end{document}C1(S2), and thus \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_\eta \circ \chi _\eta \in \operatorname {H}_{\textrm{sym}}^{\alpha }(\mathbb {S}^2)$$\end{document}Hη∘χη∈Hsymα(S2) is well-defined. Furthermore, the dependency of the mapping (3.4) on second-order derivatives of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta $$\end{document}η is linear.

The differentiability near \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta =0$$\end{document}η=0 can then be straightforwardly checked. See, for instance, [43, Lemma 2.1] or [1, Lemma 2.8]. The form of the derivative is an immediate consequence of the explicit formula (3.4). \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Next, we study the regularity of the jump term and compute its derivative. For further reference, start by recalling that the curl of a vector field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A = A_se_s+A_{\theta }e_{\theta }+A_{\varphi }e_{\varphi }$$\end{document}A=Ases+Aθeθ+Aφeφ on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^3$$\end{document}R3 reads in spherical coordinates3.6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \textrm{curl} \,A&= \frac{1}{s \sin \theta }\left( \frac{\partial }{\partial \theta }\left( A_{\varphi } \sin \theta \right) -\frac{\partial A_\theta }{\partial \varphi }\right) e_s\\&\quad +\frac{1}{s}\left( \frac{1}{\sin \theta } \frac{\partial A_s}{\partial \varphi }-\frac{\partial }{\partial s}\left( s A_{\varphi }\right) \right) e_\theta \\&\quad +\frac{1}{s}\left( \frac{\partial }{\partial s}\left( s A_\theta \right) -\frac{\partial A_s}{\partial \theta }\right) e_\varphi . \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}curlA=1ssinθ∂∂θAφsinθ-∂Aθ∂φes+1s1sinθ∂As∂φ-∂∂ssAφeθ+1s∂∂ssAθ-∂As∂θeφ.The main result of this subsection is the following.

Proof

For our analysis, it will be beneficial to extend the shape function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta $$\end{document}η to all of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^3$$\end{document}R3. For this, we consider its harmonic extension \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\eta }$$\end{document}η¯, solving \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta \bar{\eta }= 0$$\end{document}Δη¯=0 in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^3\setminus \mathbb {S}^2$$\end{document}R3\S2 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\eta }=\eta $$\end{document}η¯=η on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {S}^2$$\end{document}S2. We localize again with the help of a cut-off function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\zeta $$\end{document}ζ, that we choose smooth and radially symmetric, supported in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_2(0)$$\end{document}B2(0) and equal to 1 in a neighborhood of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_1(0)$$\end{document}B1(0). By an abuse of notation, we set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta = \zeta \bar{\eta }$$\end{document}η=ζη¯, and we obtain \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta \in \operatorname {H}^{\alpha +\frac{5}{2}}(\mathbb {R}^3\setminus \mathbb {S}^2)$$\end{document}η∈Hα+52(R3\S2) and3.8\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert \eta \Vert _{\operatorname {H}^{\alpha +{\frac{5}{2}}}(\mathbb {R}^3\setminus \mathbb {S}^2)} \le C \Vert \eta \Vert _{\operatorname {H}^{\alpha +2}(\mathbb {S}^2)}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖η‖Hα+52(R3\S2)≤C‖η‖Hα+2(S2),for some constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C = C(\alpha )>0$$\end{document}C=C(α)>0, by construction and elliptic regularity estimates, see [39, Chapter 2, Thm. 5.4].

Having now a globally extended shape function, we may also extend our parametrization to all of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^3$$\end{document}R3 by setting\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \chi _\eta (x) = (1+\eta (x))x, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}χη(x)=(1+η(x))x,for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \mathbb {R}^3$$\end{document}x∈R3. The resulting map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi _{\eta }:\mathbb {R}^3\rightarrow \mathbb {R}^3$$\end{document}χη:R3→R3 is a diffeomorphism because it is a small \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {C}^1$$\end{document}C1-perturbation of the identity after possibly reducing the radius \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_0$$\end{document}c0 introduced in Lemma 3.5. More precisely, we calculate\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textrm{D}\chi _\eta = (1+\eta ) {{\,\textrm{Id}\,}}+ x \otimes \nabla \eta , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Dχη=(1+η)Id+x⊗∇η,and thus, by the matrix determinant lemma, we have the formula\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \det (\textrm{D}\chi _\eta ) = (1+\eta )^2(1+\eta +x \cdot \nabla \eta ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}det(Dχη)=(1+η)2(1+η+x·∇η).Apparently, the determinant is positive if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert \eta \Vert _{\operatorname {C}^1}$$\end{document}‖η‖C1 is sufficiently small. This is guaranteed by the standard (fractional) Sobolev embedding in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^3$$\end{document}R3 and the bound in (3.8), if the constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_0$$\end{document}c0 in Lemma 3.5 is chosen sufficiently small.

We will now study the differentiability of the inner and outer problems separately.

The inner problem. Differentiability is now proved analogously to the homogeneous problem (3.9). This time, we consider the functional\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ N(\eta ,\tilde{\phi }) = \left( -\tilde{\nabla }\cdot \left( \tilde{M}_{\tilde{\eta }}\tilde{\nabla }\tilde{\phi }\right) +\frac{\tilde{x}}{|\tilde{x}|^2} \cdot \tilde{M}_{\tilde{\eta }} \tilde{\nabla }\tilde{\phi }+ |\tilde{x}| \tilde{\nabla }\cdot \left( \frac{1}{|\tilde{x}|^3}\tilde{m}_{\tilde{\eta }}\tilde{x}\tilde{\phi }\right) , \tilde{\phi }|_{\mathbb {S}^2}-\tilde{h}_{\tilde{\eta }}\right) . $$\end{document}N(η,ϕ~)=-∇~·M~η~∇~ϕ~+x~|x~|2·M~η~∇~ϕ~+|x~|∇~·1|x~|3m~η~x~ϕ~,ϕ~|S2-h~η~.It is well-defined and continuously Fréchet differentiable as a mapping from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {M}^{\alpha +2}\times \operatorname {H}^{\alpha +\frac{5}{2}}(B)$$\end{document}Mα+2×Hα+52(B) to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {H}^{\alpha +\frac{1}{2}}(B)\times \operatorname {H}^{\alpha +2}(\mathbb {S}^2)$$\end{document}Hα+12(B)×Hα+2(S2). Transforming back to the original problem, we get the desired result for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi ^{\text {out}}.$$\end{document}φout.

Properties of the linear operator

We start with the discussion of the invertibility of the surface tension term. It will be crucial when analyzing the regime of large surface tensions.

Lemma 3.8

For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \ge 0$$\end{document}α≥0 the operator\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} -\left( \Delta _{\mathbb {S}^2}+2{{\,\textrm{Id}\,}}\right) :T_0\mathcal {M}^{\alpha +2} \rightarrow \operatorname {H}_{\textrm{sym}}^{\alpha }(\mathbb {S}^2) /_\textrm{const}\end{aligned}$$\end{document}-ΔS2+2Id:T0Mα+2→Hsymα(S2)/constis an isomorphism.

Proof

With respect to the orthonormal basis \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{ Y_l^0: l \in \mathbb {N}_0, \ l \ne 1 \}$$\end{document}{Yl0:l∈N0,l≠1} the operator is the multiplication operator with nonzero symbol \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-(l+2)(l-1)$$\end{document}-(l+2)(l-1) and thus invertible with a loss of two derivatives. Note that the zero mean condition matches the fact that we take the quotient space with respect to constant functions in the image space. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

We turn our attention to the full operator. For notational convenience, we introduce3.26\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} [\mathcal {A}(\mu )](\delta \eta )&=\frac{2}{9\gamma } \langle \left. \textrm{D}_{\eta }\right| _{\eta =0} \mathcal {F}(\gamma ,\gamma ,\eta ),\delta \eta \rangle \\&= \sin \theta \,{e_{\varphi }\cdot }(2{{\,\textrm{Id}\,}}-\Lambda )(\sin \theta \, \delta \eta \, {e_{\varphi }}) -\mu (\Delta _{\mathbb {S}^2}+2 {{\,\textrm{Id}\,}})\delta \eta , \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}[A(μ)](δη)=29γ⟨Dηη=0F(γ,γ,η),δη⟩=sinθeφ·(2Id-Λ)(sinθδηeφ)-μ(ΔS2+2Id)δη,where we write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu = 2/(9\gamma )$$\end{document}μ=2/(9γ) in the following. For our analysis, it will be beneficial to rewrite the linear operator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {A}$$\end{document}A with the help of spherical harmonics. This can be achieved by expressing the function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta \eta $$\end{document}δη introduced in Lemma 3.5 in terms of spherical harmonics. According to the characterisation (3.3) and because \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta \eta $$\end{document}δη has zero mean, we have the expansion\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \delta \eta (\theta ) = \sum _{k=1}^{\infty } v_k Y_{2k}^0(\theta ), $$\end{document}δη(θ)=∑k=1∞vkY2k0(θ),for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_k \in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}vk∈R. Precise properties of the coefficients will be discussed later. Next, we identify \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {A}(\mu )$$\end{document}A(μ) as an operator on sequences \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(v_k)_{k \in \mathbb {N}}$$\end{document}(vk)k∈N. For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \ge 0 $$\end{document}α≥0 we set\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \operatorname {h}^\alpha :=\left\{ v = (v_k)_{k\in \mathbb {N}}\, :\, \Vert v \Vert _{\operatorname {h}^\alpha }^2 := \sum _{k = 1}^\infty k^{2\alpha }v_k^2<\infty \right\} , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}hα:=v=(vk)k∈N:‖v‖hα2:=∑k=1∞k2αvk2<∞,which is a Banach space. Lemma 3.9

For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \frac{(2k-1)(2k+1)(2k+2)}{(4k+3)(4k+5)} \sqrt{\frac{4k+5}{4k+1}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Ak(μ)=μ(2k-1)(2k+2)-2k(2k-3)(2k-1)(4k+1)(4k-1)-(2k-1)(2k+1)(2k+2)(4k+1)(4k+3),Bk=2k(2k-3)(2k-1)(4k-3)(4k-1)4k-34k+1,Ck=(2k-1)(2k+1)(2k+2)(4k+3)(4k+5)4k+54k+1.

Proof

We discuss the linear operator term by term, starting with the simplest one, the curvature term. Recalling that spherical harmonics of degree l are eigenfunctions of the Laplace–Beltrami operator for the eigenvalue \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-l(l+1)$$\end{document}-l(l+1), we observe that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ (\Delta _{\mathbb {S}^2}+2{{\,\textrm{Id}\,}})Y_{2k}^0 = - (2k-1)(2k+2) Y_{2k}^0. $$\end{document}(ΔS2+2Id)Y2k0=-(2k-1)(2k+2)Y2k0.We now address the term that involves the Dirichlet-to-Neumann operator. Proof

We shall further split\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {A}(\mu )=\mu \mathcal {A}^1-\mathcal {A}^2, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}A(μ)=μA1-A2,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {A}^1$$\end{document}A1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {A}^2$$\end{document}A2 denote the parts of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {A}$$\end{document}A that are linear respectively constant in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu $$\end{document}μ. We start with observing that both \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {A}^1$$\end{document}A1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {A}^2$$\end{document}A2 are symmetric operators, that is,3.29\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \langle v ,\mathcal {A}^i w\rangle = \langle \mathcal {A}^i v,w\rangle ,\quad \text{ for } \text{ any } v,w\in \operatorname {h}^{\alpha +2}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⟨v,Aiw⟩=⟨Aiv,w⟩,foranyv,w∈hα+2,(with the usual \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell ^2$$\end{document}ℓ2-scalar product) as a consequence of (3.27). This can also be seen on the level of functions from the symmetry of the Dirichlet-to-Neumann and Laplace–Beltrami operators. Moreover, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {A}^1$$\end{document}A1 is positive as it is given by an infinite diagonal matrix with positive entries.

We will now prove the precise statements of the proposition.

a) The operator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {A}^1$$\end{document}A1 is invertible and hence Fredholm with index 0 as a map from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {h}^{\alpha +2}$$\end{document}hα+2 to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {h}^{\alpha }$$\end{document}hα. The operator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {A}^2$$\end{document}A2 maps \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {h}^{\alpha +2}$$\end{document}hα+2 to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {h}^{\alpha +1}$$\end{document}hα+1 and is hence compact with respect to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu \mathcal {A}^1$$\end{document}μA1, because \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu \not =0$$\end{document}μ≠0 by assumption. Therefore, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu \mathcal {A}^1-\mathcal {A}^2=\mathcal {A}(\mu )$$\end{document}μA1-A2=A(μ) is a compact perturbation of a Fredholm operator of index zero and thus it is Fredholm with index 0, too. The symmetry is stated for the individual ingredients in (3.29).

b) To see that the nullspace for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu \ne 0$$\end{document}μ≠0 is at most 1-dimensional, we observe that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {A}({\mu })v=0$$\end{document}A(μ)v=0 is equivalent to the recurrence equation3.30\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} v_{k+1}={-}\frac{A_k(\mu )}{C_k}v_k{-}\frac{B_k}{C_k}v_{k-1} \quad \text { for }k\ge 2, \\ A_1(\mu )v_1+C_1v_2=0. \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}vk+1=-Ak(μ)Ckvk-BkCkvk-1fork≥2,A1(μ)v1+C1v2=0.As \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_1\ne 0$$\end{document}C1≠0, we note that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_1$$\end{document}v1 determines all other numbers \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_k$$\end{document}vk in this recursion and hence the space of solutions is 1-dimensional and might or might not be a subspace of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {h}^{\alpha +2}$$\end{document}hα+2. Indeed, the Poincaré–Perron theorem [27, Theorem 8.35] implies that there exist exactly two linearly independent solutions of the recurrence equation without the initial condition. One of these grows of factorial order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$8\mu k$$\end{document}8μk while the other decays of factorial order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-8\mu k$$\end{document}-8μk as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k \rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}k→∞. The difficulty is to understand how the initial condition, prescribed by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ A_1(\mu )v_1+C_1v_2=0$$\end{document}A1(μ)v1+C1v2=0, relates to the behavior at infinity.

Regarding the regularity statement, we notice that if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in \operatorname {h}^{\alpha +2}$$\end{document}v∈hα+2 lies in the kernel of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {A}(\mu )$$\end{document}A(μ), then3.31\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mu \mathcal {A}^1v=\mathcal {A}^2 v. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}μA1v=A2v.From the regularity properties of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {A}^2$$\end{document}A2 it follows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
